{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{ABE.SK_{i}}}}
.

•

Prepare a register
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{MSG}}}
that is initialized to
\ket{0\cdots 0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{MSG}}}}

•

To the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{\bigotimes_{i\in[k]}ABE.SK_{i}}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{MSG}}}
, apply

\bigotimes_{i\in[k]}\ket{v_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{ABE.SK_{i}}}}}\otimes\ket{w}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{MSG}}}}\rightarrow\bigotimes_{i\in[k]}\ket{v_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{ABE.SK_{i}}}}}\otimes\ket{w\oplus\MIABE.\Dec(\abe.\ct,
v_1,\cdots,v_k)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{MSG}}}}
.

•

Measure the register
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{MSG}}}
in the computational basis and output the result
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}
.

\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR^{2}}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{sk})
:

•

Parse
\mathpzc{sk}=(\rho_{i})_{i\in[k]}
. Let the register
holding
\rho_{i}
be denoted as
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{ABE.SK_{i}}}}
.

•

For each
i\in[k]
, measure the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD_{i}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{ABE.SK_{i}}}}
in the Hadamard basis to
obtain outcomes
c_{i}
and
d_{i}
.

•

Output
\mathsf{cert}=(c_{i},d_{i})_{i\in[k]}
.

\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR^{2}}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{vk}=\big(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{vk}=(x,\theta),\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,0}\oplus\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,1}\}_{i\in[k]:\theta[i]=1}\big)
and
\mathsf{cert}=(c_{i},d_{i})_{i\in[k]}
.

•

Output
\top
if
x[i]=c_{i}\oplus d_{i}\cdot(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,0}\oplus\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,1})
holds for every

i\in[k]
such that
\theta[i]=1
and
\bot
otherwise.

Let
\mathpzc{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR^{2}}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y)
.
\mathpzc{sk}
is of the form

(\rho_{i})_{i\in[k]}
, where
\rho_{i}
is of the following form, where

(x,\theta)
is the verification key of a BB84-based SKECD scheme:

\displaystyle\rho_{i}=\begin{cases}\ket{x[i]}\ket{\abe.\sk_{i,x[i]}}&if~~\theta[i]=0\\
\ket{0}\ket{\abe.\sk_{i,0}}+(-1)^{x[i]}\ket{1}\ket{\abe.\sk_{i,1}}&if~~\theta[i]=1,\end{cases}

(76)

Recall that
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,b}=\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk},i,t\|b)
for every

i\in\{2,\ldots,k\}
and
b\in\{0,1\}
. Moreover,

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{1,b}=\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk},1,t\|b\|y)
for each
b\in\{0,1\}
.

Decryption correctness.

The MI-ABE relation defined in the construction is as follows:

R(\widetilde{x}\|z,y_{1},\cdots,y_{k})
:

Let
\widetilde{x}
be
interpreted as a circuit and
z
as a
k
-input circuit.

•

Parse
y_{1}=t_{1}\|u_{1}\|y
.

•

Parse
y_{i}=t_{i}\|u_{i}
for every
i\in 2,\ldots,k
.

•

If
t_{i}\neq t_{j}
for some
i,j\in[k]
, output
1
. Otherwise, go to the next step.

•

If
z(u_{1}\|\cdots\|u_{k})=\bot
AND
\widetilde{x}(y)=0
, output
0

(decryptable). Else, output
1
.

Clearly,
z\coloneqq\widetilde{C}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pp}_{D},1)
always outputs
\bot

and
t_{1}=\ldots=t_{k}
holds by construction. Hence, the guarantee
follows from the decryption correctness of
\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}
, as long as

\widetilde{x}(y)=0
holds, as desired.

Verification correctness.

Recall that the verification only checks the Hadamard basis positions,
i.e, positions
i\in[k]
such that
\theta[i]=1
. Consider the
outcome
(c_{i},d_{i})
obtained by measuring the state

\ket{0}\ket{\abe.\sk_{i,0}}+(-1)^{x[i]}\ket{1}\ket{\abe.\sk_{i,1}}

in the Hadamard basis, where
c_{i}
denotes the first bit of the
outcome. It is easy to see that
x[i]=c_{i}\oplus d_{i}\cdot(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,0}\oplus\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,1})
is satisfied. Hence, the
verification correctness follows.

8.4
Proof of Selective IND-KLA Security

Let
\mathpzc{A}
be an adversary for the selective IND-KLA security of
\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR^{2}}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}
.
We consider the following sequence of experiments.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}
:

This is

\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR^{2}}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
.

1.

\mathpzc{A}
declares the challenge ciphertext attribute
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
.

2.

The challenger
\mathpzc{Ch}
generates

(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and

\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
, and sends
\mathsf{ek}\coloneqq\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
can get access to the following (stateful) oracles, where
the list
L_{\mathpzc{KG}}
used by the oracles is initialized to an empty
list:

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(y)
:

Given
y
, it finds an entry of the form

(y,\mathsf{vk}_{y},V_{y})
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. If there is such an entry, it returns
\bot
.
Otherwise, it generates
(\mathpzc{sk}_{y},\mathsf{vk}_{y})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y)
,
sends
\mathpzc{sk}_{y}
to
\mathpzc{A}
, and adds
(y,\mathsf{vk}_{y},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Vrfy}}}(y,\mathsf{cert})
:

Given
(y,\mathsf{cert})
, it finds an
entry
(y,\mathsf{vk}_{y},V_{y})
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. (If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
.)
It then runs
d\coloneqq\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{y},\mathsf{cert})
and returns

d
to
\mathpzc{A}
. If
V_{y}=\bot
, it updates the entry into

(y,\mathsf{vk}_{y},d)
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to the challenger. If

V_{j}=\bot
for some
j\in[q]
,
\mathpzc{Ch}
outputs
0
as the final
output of this experiment. Otherwise,
\mathpzc{Ch}
generates

\widetilde{C}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pp}_{D},1)
and

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk},x^{*}\|\widetilde{C}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{coin}}^{*})
, and sends

\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
.
\mathpzc{Ch}
outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}
except
that
\widetilde{C}^{*}
is generated as
\widetilde{C}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},D[\mathsf{lock}\oplus r,\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk}],\mathsf{lock},0)
, where
\mathsf{lock}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
,
r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and

D[\mathsf{lock}\oplus r,\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk}](x)
is a circuit that has
\mathsf{lock}\oplus r
and
\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk}
hardwired and outputs
\mathsf{lock}\oplus r\oplus\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{CDec}(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},x)
.

Since
\mathsf{lock}
is chosen at random independently of all other
variables, from the security of compute-and-compare obfuscation, we
have that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}\approx\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
except
that
\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct}
generated as part of
\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR^{2}}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y)

such that
x^{*}(y)=0
, is generated as

(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct},\allowbreak\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},r)
.

In the previous step, we changed the distribution of
\widetilde{C}^{*}
used
to generate the challenge ciphertext so that
\widetilde{C}^{*}
has

\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk}
hardwired. However, the ciphertext is given to
\mathpzc{A}

after
\mathpzc{A}
deletes all the leased secret keys satisfying the ABE
relation, and thus the
corresponding ciphertetexts of
\mathsf{SKECD}
. Thus, we can use the security of

\mathsf{SKECD}
to argue that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}\approx\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
. Let

q
be the number of key-queries made to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
that satisfy
the relation wrt
x^{*}
. Consider now
q+1
hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,0}^{\mathsf{coin}},\cdots,\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,q}^{\mathsf{coin}}
where for every
l\in[q]
,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,l}^{\mathsf{coin}}

is such that the first
l
of the
q
keys are generated using

\mathsf{SKECD}
encryptions of
r
(instead of
0^{\lambda}
). Notice that

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,0}^{\mathsf{coin}}\equiv\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
and

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,q}^{\mathsf{coin}}\equiv\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
. Suppose that

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,l}^{\mathsf{coin}}\not\approx\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,l+1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
for some
l\in[q]
. Let
\mathpzc{D}
be a distinguisher with non-negligible advantage in
distinguishing the hybrids.
We will construct the following reduction to the IND-CVA-CD
security of
\mathsf{SKECD}
:

Execution of
\mathpzc{R}{D}
in

\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKECD},\mathpzc{R}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cva}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cd}}(1^{\lambda},b)
:

1.

The challenger
\mathpzc{Ch}
computes
\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

\mathpzc{D}
declares a challenge ciphertext attribute
x^{*}
to
\mathpzc{R}
.
\mathpzc{R}
generates
(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(\allowbreak 1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{ek}\coloneqq\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{D}
.

3.

\mathpzc{R}
sends
(r_{0},r_{1})\coloneqq(0^{\lambda},r)
to
\mathpzc{Ch}
.

4.

\mathpzc{Ch}
computes
(\mathpzc{ct}^{\star},\mathsf{vk}^{\star})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},r_{b})
and sends
\mathpzc{ct}^{\star}
to
\mathpzc{R}
.

5.

\mathpzc{R}
simulates the oracle
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(y)
for
\mathpzc{D}
as
follows. Given
y
, it finds an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk}_{y},V_{y})
from

L_{\mathpzc{KG}}
. If there is such an entry, it returns
\perp
. Otherwise,
it proceeds as follows:

•

For the
j
-th query (of the
q
satisfying key-queries), if
j\neq l+1
,
\mathpzc{R}
computes

\mathpzc{sk}_{y}
and
\mathsf{vk}_{y}
as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}
, except
that the values
(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{vk})
are computed using

O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Enc}}}(r)
for
j\in[l]
and using

O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Enc}}}(0^{\lambda})
for
j\in\{l+2,\ldots,q\}
.

•

For the
j
-th query (of the
q
satisfying key-queries), if
j=l+1
,
\mathpzc{R}
computes

\mathpzc{sk}_{y}
as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}
, except that the value
\mathpzc{ct}^{\star}
is used instead
of
\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct}
. Consider the values
\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,0},\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,1}\}_{i\in[k]:\theta[i]=1}
computed during the
computation of
\mathpzc{sk}_{y}
.
\mathpzc{R}
computes the value

\widetilde{\mathsf{vk}}_{y}=\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,0}\oplus\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,1}\}_{i\in[k]}
.

•

If
x^{*}(y)\neq 0
(unsatisfying key-queries),
\mathpzc{R}
computes

\mathpzc{sk}_{y}
and
\mathsf{vk}_{y}
as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}
, except that
(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{vk})
are computed using
O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Enc}}}(0^{\lambda})
.

6.

\mathpzc{R}
simulates the view of oracle
O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Vrfy}}}(y,\mathsf{cert})
for

\mathpzc{D}
as follows. Given
(y,\mathsf{cert})
, it finds an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk}_{y},V_{y})
from
L_{\mathpzc{KG}}
. If there is no such entry, it returns
\perp
.
Otherwise, it proceeds as follows:

•

If
x^{*}(y)\neq 0
,
\mathpzc{R}
simulates access to
O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Vrfy}}}
as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}
.

•

If
y
corresponds to the
j
-th satisfying key-query to

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and
j\neq l+1
,

\mathpzc{R}
simulates access to
O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Vrfy}}}
as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}
.

•

If
y
corresponds to the
j
-th satisfying key-query to

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and
j=l+1
,
\mathpzc{R}
simulates access to

O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Vrfy}}}(y,\mathsf{cert})
as follows:

(a)

Parse
\widetilde{\mathsf{vk}}_{y}=\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,0}\oplus\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,1}\}_{i\in[k]}
and
\mathsf{cert}=(c_{i},d_{i})_{i\in[k]}
.

(b)

Compute
x[i]=c_{i}\oplus d_{i}\cdot(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,0}\oplus\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,1})
for every
i\in[k]
.

(c)

If
O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Vrfy}}}(x)=\mathsf{sk}
, set
d\coloneqq\top
. Else,
set
d\coloneqq\bot
.

It returns
d
to
\mathpzc{D}
. If
V_{y}=\bot
, it updates the entry in

L_{\mathpzc{KG}}
into
(y,\mathsf{vk}_{y},d)
.

7.

\mathpzc{D}
sends
(m_{0}^{*},m_{1}^{*})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to
\mathpzc{R}
.
\mathpzc{R}

computes
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
as in Step
4.
of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
using

\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk}\coloneqq\mathsf{sk}
obtained from
\mathpzc{Ch}
. It sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to

\mathpzc{D}
if
V_{y}=\top
for every entry of the form
(y,\mathsf{vk}_{y},V_{y})
in

L_{\mathpzc{KG}}
such that
x^{*}(y)=0
. Else, it outputs
\bot
.

8.

\mathpzc{D}
guesses a bit
b^{\prime}
.
\mathpzc{R}
sends
b^{\prime}
to
\mathpzc{Ch}
.

Notice that the view of
\mathpzc{D}
in the reduction is that of

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,l}^{\mathsf{coin}}
if
b=0
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,l+1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
if
b=1
.
Moreover,
\mathpzc{D}
can only distinguish when
V_{y}=\top
for all entries

(y,\mathsf{vk}_{y},V_{y})\in L_{\mathpzc{KG}}
such that
x^{*}(y)=0
.
This means that
V_{y}
corresponding to the
(l+1)
-th satisfying
query to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
also
satisfies
V_{y}=\top
. Consequently, the
corresponding verification check of
\mathpzc{Ch}
is also
\top
. Hence,
\mathpzc{R}
succeeds
in breaking IND-CVA-CD security of
\mathsf{SKECD}
. Therefore, we have

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}\approx\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
.

We will now bound the distinguishing gap between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{0}
and

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{1}
using the security of
\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}
. Consider a
decryption key
\mathpzc{sk}_{y}=(\rho_{i})_{i\in[k]}
such that
x^{*}(y)=0
. Recall that a ciphertext of BB84-based
\mathsf{SKECD}
is of the form

\ket{\psi_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\ket{\psi_k}
, where

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_i}=\begin{cases}\ket{x[i]}&\textrm{if}~~\theta[i]=0\\
\ket{0}+(-1)^{x[i]}\ket{1}&\textrm{if}~~\theta[i]=1\end{cases}

(77)

As a result, we have

\displaystyle\rho_{i}=\begin{cases}\ket{x[i]}\ket{\abe.\sk_{i,x[i]}}&\textrm{if}~~\theta[i]=0\\
\ket{0}\ket{\abe.\sk_{i,0}}+(-1)^{x[i]}\ket{1}\ket{\abe.\sk_{i,1}}&\textrm{if}~~\theta[i]=1,\end{cases}

(78)

where
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{1,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk},1,t_{y}\|b\|y)
for each

b\in\{0,1\}
and

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk},i,\allowbreak t_{y}\|b)

for each
i\in\{2,\ldots,k\}
and
b\in\{0,1\}
. Here,
t_{y}
is a
random value that is common for each of the
k
“slots” of the
decryption-key
\mathpzc{sk}_{y}
. Notice first that no
two values
t_{y},t_{w}
are equal for
t_{w}
corresponding to some

\mathpzc{sk}_{w}
except with negligible probability.
Consequently, due to the defined relation
R
and the selective
security of
\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}
, any set of
k
secret keys is “decryptable” only if
they correspond to the same decryption key
\mathpzc{sk}_{y}
. Now, we notice
that
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,1-x[i]}
is not given to
\mathpzc{A}
for any
i\in[k]

such that
\theta[i]=0
. This means that for any set of
k

\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}

keys corresponding to the decryption-key
\mathpzc{sk}_{y}
, the attributes

(x^{\prime}[1],\cdots,x^{\prime}[k])
satisfy
x^{\prime}[i]=x[i]
for all
i:\theta[i]=0
. Consequently, the classical decryption property (See Definition

3.12
) of the BB84-based SKE-CD scheme guarantees that
\mathsf{lock}\oplus r\oplus\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{CDec}(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},x^{\prime})=\mathsf{lock}
in these hybrids. This means that

\widetilde{C}^{*}(x^{\prime})=0
(instead of
\bot
). As a result, any subset of

\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}
keys of size
k
given to
\mathpzc{A}
is a “non-decryptable” set
in the hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{1}
.
It is easy to see now that we can reduce to the selective security of

\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}
. Specifically, the reduction specifies the target attribute

x^{*}\|\widetilde{C}^{*}
where

\widetilde{C}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},D[\mathsf{lock}\oplus r,\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk}],\mathsf{lock},0)

and simulates the view for
\mathpzc{A}
by querying the key-generation oracle
of
\mathsf{MI}\textrm{-}\mathsf{ABE}
accordingly. Note that the reduction can handle
verification queries since it can obtain both
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,0}
and

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,1}
for every
i\in[k]
such that
\theta[i]=1
that are
sufficient to perform the verification. (Especially, it does not need

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i,1-x[i]}
for
i\in[k]
such that
\theta[i]=0
for
verification.) We now state the following theorem:

Theorem 8.7.

There exists an ABE-CR
2
-SKL scheme satisfying
selective IND-KLA Security, assuming the existence of a
selectively-secure Multi-Input ABE scheme for polynomial-arity,
Compute-and-Compare Obfuscation, and a BB84-based SKE-CD scheme.

Appendix A
Quantum Secure ABE for All Relations from LWE

We show there exists quantum selective-secure ABE for all relations computable in polynomial time based on the LWE assumption.

A.1
Preparation

Definition A.1 (Quantum-Accessible Pseudo-Random Function).

Let
\{\mathsf{PRF}_{k}:\{0,1\}^{\ell_{1}}\rightarrow\allowbreak\{0,1\}^{\ell_{2}}\mid k\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}\}
be a family of polynomially computable functions, where
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are some polynomials of
\lambda
.
We say that
\mathsf{PRF}
is a quantum-accessible pseudo-random function (QPRF) family if for any QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{prf}}(\lambda)=\absolutevalue{\Pr[\qA^{\ket{\PRF_{k}(\cdot)}}(1^\secp) \gets 1 \mid k \gets\bit^{\secp}]
-\Pr[\qA^{\ket{\sfR(\cdot)}}(1^\secp) \gets 1 \mid\sfR\gets\cU]
}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda),

(79)

where
\mathcal{U}
is the set of all functions from
\{0,1\}^{\ell_{1}}
to
\{0,1\}^{\ell_{2}}
.

Theorem A.2 (
[
Zha12
]
).

If there exists a OWF, there exists a QPRF.

A.2
Proofs

We first define Key-Policy ABE for polynomial size circuits and briefly see that it can be used to instantiate ABE for any relations computable in polynomial time, even under quantum selective-security.
Then, we prove that the Key-Policy ABE scheme for polynomial size circuits by Boneh et al.
[
BGG+14
]
with a light modification using QPRF satisfies quantum selective-security under the LWE assumption.

Key-Policy ABE for Circuits:

Let
\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}=\{0,1\}^{n(\lambda)}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda}
be the set of all circuits with input space
\{0,1\}^{n(\lambda)}
and size at most
s(\lambda)
, where
n
and
s
are some polynomials.
Let
R_{\lambda}
be the following relation:

R_{\lambda}(x,y)=0\iff y(x)=0

An ABE scheme for such
\{\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda},\{\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda}
,
and
\{R_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda}
is referred to as a Key-Policy ABE scheme
for circuits.

Lemma A.3.

If there exists a quantum selective-secure Key-Policy ABE scheme
for circuits, then there exists a quantum selective-secure ABE scheme for all relations computable in polynomial time.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{n}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, and
R_{\lambda}:\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}\times\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
, where
n
and
\ell
are polynomials and
R_{\lambda}
is computable in polynomial time.
We construct ABE scheme
\mathsf{ABE}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
with attribute spaces
\{\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}\}
and
\{\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda}\}
and relation
\{R_{\lambda}\}
, using Key-Policy ABE scheme for circuits
\mathsf{ABE}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KG}^{\prime},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
with the following attribute spaces
\{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{\lambda}\}
and
\{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{\lambda}\}
.

•

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{\lambda}=\{0,1\}^{n}
.

•

Let
C_{\lambda,y}
be the circuit such that
C_{\lambda,y}(x)=R_{\lambda}(x,y)
for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
y\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, and let
s
be the maximum size of
C_{y}
. Note that
s
is a polynomial in
\lambda
. Then,
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
is the set of all circuits with input length
n
and size at most
s^{\prime}
.

The scheme is as follows:

\mathsf{KG}^{\prime}(\mathsf{msk},y,r)=\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{msk},C_{\lambda,y},r)
.
It is easy to see that the correctness of
\mathsf{ABE}
follows from that of
\mathsf{ABE}^{\prime}
. For the
quantum selective security of
\mathsf{ABE}^{\prime}
, consider a reduction
\mathpzc{R}

to the quantum selective security of
\mathsf{ABE}
.

Execution of
\mathpzc{R}^{\mathpzc{A}}
in Experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{ABE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{q}\mbox{-}\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
:

1.

\mathpzc{R}
receives challenge attribute
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}
from
\mathpzc{A}
and
forwards it to the challenger
\mathsf{Ch}
.

2.

For each oracle query
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{Y}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{Z}}})
made by
\mathpzc{A}
,

\mathpzc{R}
performs the following map on
register
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{Z}}}
initialized to
\ket{0^{s(\secp)}}
:

\ket{y}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{Y}}}}\ket{z}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{Z}}}}\mapsto\ket{y}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{Y}}}}\ket{z \xor C_{\secp,y}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{Z}}}}

3.

Then,
\mathpzc{R}
queries the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{Y}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{Z}}}
to

O_{\mathtt{qkg}}
followed by returning the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{Y}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{Z}}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1})
to
\mathpzc{R}
,
\mathpzc{R}
forwards it to

\mathsf{Ch}
.

5.

On receiving
\mathsf{ct}^{\star}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x^{*},\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{coin}})
from

\mathsf{Ch}
,
\mathpzc{R}
forwards it to
\mathpzc{A}
.

6.

Finally, when
\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
,
\mathpzc{R}
sends

\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{Ch}
.

Since the view of
\mathpzc{A}
is identical to that in the
quantum selective security experiment for scheme
\mathsf{ABE}^{\prime}
,
\mathpzc{R}
ends
up breaking the quantum selective security of
\mathsf{ABE}
.
∎

Theorem A.4.

Assuming the polynomial hardness of the
LWE problem, there exists a quantum selective-secure
Key-Policy ABE scheme for circuits.

Proof.

We claim that the Key-Policy ABE scheme for circuits by Boneh et al.

[
BGG+14
]
based on LWE is quantum selective-secure.
Actually, we will alter the key-generation algorithm of their scheme
as follows:
\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y,k)=\mathsf{KG}^{\prime}(\mathsf{msk},y,\mathsf{PRF}_{k}(y))
where

\mathsf{KG}^{\prime}
is the key-generation algorithm of their construction with
explicit random coins
\mathsf{PRF}_{k}(y)
, and
\{\mathsf{PRF}_{k}\}_{k}
is QPRF.
This is a common technique utilized in quantum security proofs (See
for Eg.
[
BZ13a
,
BZ13b
]
) that allows one to use a
common random value for every term of a superposition. In the
following discussion, whenever we discuss a hybrid titled
"
\mathsf{Game}\;i
" for some value
i
, it refers to the
corresponding hybrid in Theorem 4.2 of
[
BGG+14
]
. Also, any
indistinguishability claims between
\mathsf{Game}
hybrids that are
mentioned to be previously established, will refer to their work.
Consider the following sequence of hybrids for the aforementioned
ABE scheme
\mathsf{ABE}
and a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
:

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}
:

This is the same as the experiment

\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{ABE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{q}\mbox{-}\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
:

This is similar to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}
, except
that the public-key
\mathsf{pk}
is generated based on the challenge
attribute
x^{*}
, as in the hybrid
\mathsf{Game}\;1
. It was shown
that the hybrids
\mathsf{Game}\;0
(the original experiment) and

\mathsf{Game}\;1
are statistically indistinguishable. By the same
argument, it follows that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}\approx_{s}\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
:

This is similar to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
, except
that the public-key is further modified as in
\mathsf{Game}\;2
.
Unlike the change made in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
though, this change
requires the key-queries to be answered differently as in

\mathsf{Game}\;2
. More specifically, the hybrid now has a
punctured master secret-key that still allows it to simulate
every key-query
y
such that
C_{y}(x^{*})\neq\bot
. It was shown
previously that
\mathsf{Game}\;1\approx_{s}\mathsf{Game}\;2
.
Consider now the intermediate hybrids
\widetilde{\mathsf{Hyb}}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}},\widetilde{\mathsf{Hyb}}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
that are similar to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}},\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
respectively, except that all the superposition
key-queries in these hybrids are responded using independent (true)
randomness in every term of the superposition. We will now restate
the following lemma by
[
BZ13a
]
, which we will use to
argue that
\widetilde{\mathsf{Hyb}}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}\approx_{s}\widetilde{\mathsf{Hyb}}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
.

Lemma A.5.

[
BZ13a
]

Let
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
be sets and for each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, let
D_{y}
and

D^{\prime}_{y}
be distributions on
\mathcal{Z}
such that
\mathsf{SD}(D_{y},D^{\prime}_{y})\leq\epsilon
. Let
O:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
and
O^{\prime}:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
be functions
such that
O(y)
outputs
z\leftarrow D_{y}
and
O^{\prime}(y)
outputs
z^{\prime}\leftarrow D^{\prime}_{y}
. Then,
O(y)
and
O^{\prime}(y)
are
\epsilon^{\prime}
-statistically
indistinguishable by quantum algorithms making
q
superposition
oracle queries, such that
\epsilon^{\prime}=\sqrt{8C_{0}q^{3}\epsilon}
where

C_{0}
is a constant.

Recall that
\mathcal{Y}
denotes the set of key-attributes. Let us fix a
challenge ciphertext attribute
x^{*}
for the following discussion.
For each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
let the distributions
D^{1}_{y}[\mathsf{pk}],D^{2}_{y}[\mathsf{pk}]

correspond to how a key for attribute
y
is sampled in the hybrids

\widetilde{\mathsf{Hyb}}^{\mathsf{coin}}_{1},\widetilde{\mathsf{Hyb}}^{\mathsf{coin}}_{2}
respectively,
conditioned on the public key being
\mathsf{pk}
. Note that for each
i\in[2]
, we consider
D_{y}^{i}[\mathsf{pk}]
to also output the public-key
\mathsf{pk}

along with the secret-key for
y
. We know that on average over

\mathsf{pk}
,
D_{y}^{1}[\mathsf{pk}]\approx_{s}D_{y}^{2}[\mathsf{pk}]
holds for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
.
It now follows from the above lemma that on average over
\mathsf{pk}
, the
analogously defined oracles
O_{1}[\mathsf{pk}],O_{2}[\mathsf{pk}]
are statistically
indistinguishable by algorithms making polynomially many
superposition queries. Observe that with access to oracle
O_{i}[\mathsf{pk}]

for each
i\in[2]
, the view of
\mathpzc{A}
in

\widetilde{\mathsf{Hyb}}^{\mathsf{coin}}_{i}
(conditioned on the public-key being

\mathsf{pk}
) can easily
be recreated as the oracle outputs
\mathsf{pk}
, which can then be used to
compute the challenge ciphertext corresponding to
\mathsf{coin}
.
Consequently, it follows that the hybrids
\widetilde{\mathsf{Hyb}}^{\mathsf{coin}}_{1}

and
\widetilde{\mathsf{Hyb}}^{\mathsf{coin}}_{2}
are statistically indistinguishable.
Since
\widetilde{\mathsf{Hyb}}^{\mathsf{coin}}_{i}\approx\mathsf{Hyb}^{\mathsf{coin}}_{i}
holds for each

i\in[2]
(by the quantum-security of
\mathsf{PRF}
), we have that

\mathsf{Hyb}^{\mathsf{coin}}_{1}\approx\mathsf{Hyb}^{\mathsf{coin}}_{2}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{\mathsf{coin}}
:

This is similar to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
, except
that the challenge ciphertext is chosen uniformly at random, as in

\mathsf{Game}\;3
.

Observe that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{0}\equiv\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{1}
. It was shown previously
that
\mathsf{Game}\;3\approx\mathsf{Game}\;2
by a reduction to
LWE. Specifically, the reduction prepares the setup based on the LWE
sample and the challenge ciphertext
x^{*}
, and plants the LWE
challenge in the challenge ciphertext. It is easy to see that a
similar reduction works in our case, thereby showing that

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}\approx\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{\mathsf{coin}}
. Consequently, it follows that

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{0}\approx\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{1}
.

This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
IND-CVA-CD Secure BB84 Based SKE-CD

To prove
Theorem

3.13
, we show how to transform IND-CD secure SKE-CD to IND-CVA-CD secure one.

Definition B.1 (IND-CD Security).

We define the security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKECD},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cd}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
in the same way as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKECD},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cva}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cd}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
except that the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
is allowed to get access to the verification oracle only once.
We say that
\mathsf{SKECD}
is IND-CD secure if for any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SKECD},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cd}}(1^{\lambda})\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[
\expb{\CDSKE,\qA}{ind}{cd}(1^\secp, 0)=1] - \Pr[
\expb{\CDSKE,\qA}{ind}{cd}(1^\secp, 1)=1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(80)

Bartusek and Khurana
[
BK23
]
showed the following theorem.

Theorem B.2 (
[
BK23
]
).

There exists an IND-CD secure BB84 based SKE-CD scheme assuming just an IND-CPA secure SKE scheme.

We prove
Theorem

3.13
by proving the following theorem.

Theorem B.3.

Let
\mathsf{SKECD}=(\mathsf{KG},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a BB84-based SKE-CD scheme.
If
\mathsf{SKECD}
is IND-CD secure, then it is also IND-CVA-CD secure.

Proof.

Let
\mathpzc{A}
be a QPT adversary that attacks the IND-CVA-CD security of
\mathsf{SKECD}
with
q
verification queries.
Let
\mathtt{FV}_{i}
be the event that the
i
-th verification query is the first verification query such that the answer for it is not
\bot
.
Then,
\mathpzc{A}
’s advantage can be described as follows.

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SKECD},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cva}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cd}}(1^{\lambda})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\absolutevalue{
\Pr[\expc{\CDSKE,\qA}{ind}{cva}{cd}(1^\secp, 0)=1]
-\Pr[\expc{\CDSKE,\qA}{ind}{cva}{cd}(1^\secp, 1)=1]
}

(81)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in[q]}\absolutevalue{
\Pr[\expc{\CDSKE,\qA}{ind}{cva}{cd}(1^\secp, 0)=1 \land\FV_i]
-\Pr[\expc{\CDSKE,\qA}{ind}{cva}{cd}(1^\secp, 1)=1 \land\FV_i]\label{eqn-SKECD-BB84}
}

(82)

To bound each term of
Equation

82
, we construct the following QPT adversary
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
that attacks the IND-CD security of
\mathsf{SKECD}
using
\mathpzc{A}
.

1.

\mathpzc{B}_{i}
initializes
\mathpzc{A}
with the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
makes queries to the encryption oracle throughout the experiment.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Enc}}}(\mathsf{m})
:

When
\mathpzc{A}
makes an encryption query
\mathsf{m}
,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
forwards it to its own encryption oracle, and sends back the answer
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})
from the encryption oracle to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

When
\mathpzc{A}
outputs
(\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
sends
(\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1})
to its challenger. On receiving the challenge ciphertext
\mathpzc{ct}^{*}
from the challenger,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
measures its classical part
\mathsf{cla}
. This does not affect
\mathpzc{ct}^{*}
.
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
then forwards
\mathpzc{ct}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

Hereafter,
\mathpzc{A}
can get access to the following oracle.

O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Vrfy}}}(\mathsf{cert}_{j})
:

For the
j
-th query
\mathsf{cert}_{j}
, if
j<i
,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
returns
\bot
to
\mathpzc{A}
. if
j=i
,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
queries
\mathsf{cert}_{j}
to its verification oracle. If the response is
\bot
,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
aborts. Otherwise if the answer is
\mathsf{sk}
,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
forwards
\mathsf{sk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
also computes
\theta
from
\mathsf{cla}
and
\mathsf{sk}
, and sets
\mathsf{vk}^{\prime}=(\theta,\mathsf{cert}_{i})
.
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
checks whether
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}^{\prime},\mathsf{cert}_{j})=\bot
holds for every
j<i
(that is, whether the
i
-th query is the first query resulting in the answer other than
\bot
). If not,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
aborts. Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
responds to the subsequent verification queries using
\mathsf{vk}^{\prime}
.

5.

When
\mathpzc{A}
outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathsf{vk}^{*}=(\theta,x)
be the verification key corresponding to
\mathpzc{ct}^{*}
. For any string
\mathsf{cert}
, if
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}^{*},\mathsf{cert})=\top
,
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}^{*},\cdot)
and
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}^{\prime},\cdot)
for
\mathsf{vk}^{\prime}=(\theta,\mathsf{cert})
are functionally equivalent, and
\mathsf{vk}^{\prime}
can be used as an alternative verification key. This is because
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}^{*},\mathsf{cert}^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}^{\prime},\mathsf{cert}^{\prime})
respectively checks whether
\mathsf{cert}^{\prime}[i]=x[i]
and
\mathsf{cert}^{\prime}[i]=\mathsf{cert}[i]
holds or not for every
i\in[n]
such that
\theta[i]=1
, and for such
i
, we have
x[i]=\mathsf{cert}[i]
from the fact that
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}^{*},\mathsf{cert})=\top
.

From the above, after the
i
-th verification query from
\mathpzc{A}
is responded,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
can check whether its simulation of
\mathpzc{A}
so far has been successful or not. Moreover, if the simulation has failed,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
aborts, and otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}_{i}
can successfully simulate the remaining steps for
\mathpzc{A}
using the alternative verification key
\mathsf{vk}^{\prime}=(\theta,\mathsf{cert}_{i})
.
Then, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKECD},\mathpzc{B}_{i}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cd}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKECD},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cva}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cd}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})=1\land\mathtt{FV}_{i}]
.
Since,
\mathsf{SKECD}
satisfies IND-CD security, it holds that

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\CDSKE,\qB_i}{ind}{cd}(1^\secp, 0)=1]-\Pr[\expb{\CDSKE,\qB_i}{ind}{cd}(1^\secp, 1)=1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

(84)

for every
i\in[q]
, which shows each term of
Equation

82
is negligible.
This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Construction of SKFE-CR-SKL with Key Testability

C.1
Construction

We construct an SKFE-CR-SKL scheme for the functionality
F:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
with key testability
\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}=\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\allowbreak\mathpzc{Vrfy})
having the
additional algorithms
\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.(\mathsf{CDec},\mathsf{KeyTest})
, using the
following building blocks.

•

BB84-based SKE-CD scheme (Definition
3.12
)
\mathsf{SKECD}=\mathsf{SKECD}.(\mathsf{KG},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\allowbreak\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
having the classical
decryption algorithm
\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{CDec}
.

•

Classical SKFE scheme
\mathsf{SKFE}=\mathsf{SKFE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
for the functionality
F:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
.

•

OWF
f:\{0,1\}^{\lambda}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{p(\lambda)}
for some polynomial
p
.

The construction is as follows:

\mathsf{SKE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

1.

Generate
\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Generate
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

3.

Output
\mathsf{msk}\coloneqq(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk})
.

\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y)
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{msk}=(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk})
.

2.

Generate
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},y)
.

3.

Generate

(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y})
.
Recall that
\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{vk}
is of the form

(x,\theta)\in\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}\times\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}
, and

\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct}
is of the form

\ket{\psi_1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{1}}}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\ket{\psi_{\ctlen}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}}}}}
.

4.

Generate
s_{i,b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and compute
t_{i,b}\leftarrow f(s_{i,b})
for every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
.
Set
T\coloneqq t_{1,0}\|t_{1,1}\|\cdots\|t_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}},0}\|t_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}},1}
and
S=\{s_{i,0}\oplus s_{i,1}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]\;:\;\theta[i]=1}
.

5.

Prepare a register
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
that is initialized to

\ket{0^\secp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}}
for every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
.

6.

For every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
, apply the map

\displaystyle\ket{u_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}}\otimes\ket{v_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}}\rightarrow\ket{u_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}}\otimes\ket{v_i\oplus s_{i,u_i}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}}

(85)

to the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
and obtain the resulting state
\rho_{i}
.

7.

Output
\mathpzc{sk}_{y}=(\rho_{i})_{i\in{[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]}}
,

\mathsf{vk}=(x,\theta,S)
, and
\mathsf{tk}=T
.

\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},x)
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{msk}=(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk})
.

2.

Generate
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},x)
.

3.

Output
\mathsf{ct}\coloneqq(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct})
.

\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{CDec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct})
. Parse
\mathsf{sk}
as a string over
the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{1}}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{1}}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}}}}
. Let

\widetilde{\mathsf{sk}}
be the sub-string of
\mathsf{sk}
on register

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT}}}
.

2.

Compute
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{CDec}(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\widetilde{\mathsf{sk}})
.

3.

Output
z\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct})
.

\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{sk},\mathsf{ct})
:

1.

Parse
(\rho_{i})_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]}
. We denote the register
holding
\rho_{i}
as
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
for
every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
.

2.

Prepare a register
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{MSG}}}
of
\ell_{\mathsf{m}}
qubits that is
initialized to
\ket{0\cdots 0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{MSG}}}}
.

3.

Apply the map

\displaystyle\ket{u}_{\bigotimes_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}}\otimes\ket{w}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{MSG}}}}\rightarrow\ket{u}_{\bigotimes_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}}\otimes\ket{w\oplus\SKFESKL.\CDec(u, \ct)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{MSG}}}}

(86)

to the registers

\bigotimes_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{MSG}}}
.

4.

Measure
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{MSG}}}
in the computational basis and output the result
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}
.

\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{sk})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{vk}=(x,\theta,S=\{s_{i,0}\oplus s_{i,1}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]\;:\;\theta[i]=1})
and

\mathpzc{sk}=(\rho_{i})_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]}
where
\rho_{i}
is a state on
the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
.

2.

For every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
, measure
\rho_{i}
in the Hadamard
basis to get outcomes
c_{i},d_{i}
corresponding to
the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
respectively.

3.

Output
\top
if
x[i]=c_{i}\oplus d_{i}\cdot(s_{i,0}\oplus s_{i,1})
holds for every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
such that
\theta[i]=1
.
Otherwise, output
\bot
.

\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{KeyTest}(\mathsf{tk},\mathsf{sk})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{sk}
as a string over the registers

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{1}}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{1}}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}}}}
.
Let
u_{i}
denote the value on

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}
and
v_{i}
the value on
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
. Parse
\mathsf{tk}

as
T=t_{1,0}\|t_{1,1}\|\cdots\|t_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}},0}\|t_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}},1}
.

2.

Let
\mathsf{Check}[t_{i,0},t_{i_{1}}](u_{i},v_{i})
be the deterministic
algorithm that outputs
1
if
f(v_{i})=t_{i,u_{i}}
holds and
0

otherwise.

3.

Output
\mathsf{Check}[t_{1,0},t_{1,1}](u_{1},v_{1})\land\mathsf{Check}[t_{2,0},t_{2,1}](u_{2},v_{2})\land\cdots\land\mathsf{Check}[t_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}},0},t_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}},1}](u_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}},v_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}})
.

C.2
Proof of Selective
Single-Ciphertext KLA Security

Let
\mathpzc{A}
be an adversary for the selective
single-ciphertext KLA security of the
construction
\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}
that makes use of a BB84-based
SKE-CD scheme
\mathsf{SKECD}
. Consider the hybrid
\mathsf{Hyb}_{j}^{\mathsf{coin}}
defined
as follows:

\mathsf{Hyb}_{j}^{\mathsf{coin}}
:

1.

Initialized with
1^{\lambda}
,
\mathpzc{A}
outputs
(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
.
Sample
\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
can get access to the following (stateful) oracles,
where the list
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
used by the oracles is initialized
to an empty list:

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(y)
:

Given
y
, it finds an entry of the form

(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. If there is such an entry, it
returns
\bot
. Otherwise it proceeds as follows:

(i)

If this is the
k
-th query for
k\leq j

and
F(x_{0}^{*},y)\neq F(x_{1}^{*},y)
, then compute
(\mathpzc{sk},\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{tk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{\widetilde{KG}}(\mathsf{msk},y)
where
\mathpzc{\widetilde{KG}}
is defined below.
Otherwise, compute
(\mathpzc{sk},\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{tk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y)
.

(ii)

It sends
\mathpzc{sk}
and
\mathsf{tk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
and adds
(y,\mathsf{vk},\bot)

to
L_{\mathpzc{KG}}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(y,\widetilde{\mathpzc{sk}})
:

Given

(y,\widetilde{\mathpzc{sk}})
, it finds an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
from

L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. (If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
.) It
then runs
d\leftarrow\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\widetilde{\mathpzc{sk}})
and returns

d
to
\mathpzc{A}
. If
V=\top
, it updates the entry into

(y,\mathsf{vk},d)
.

\mathpzc{\widetilde{KG}}(\mathsf{msk})
:

Differences from
\mathpzc{KG}
are colored in red:

(a)

Parse
\mathsf{msk}=(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk})
.

(b)

Generate
r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

(c)

Generate

(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r})
.

\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{vk}
is of the form

(x,\theta)\in\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}\times\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}
, and

\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct}
is of the form

\ket{\psi_1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{1}}}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\ket{\psi_{\ctlen}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}}}}}
.

(d)

Generate
s_{i,b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and compute
t_{i,b}\leftarrow f(s_{i,b})
for every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
.
Set
T\coloneqq t_{1,0}\|t_{1,1}\|\cdots\|t_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}},0}\|t_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}},1}
and
S=\{s_{i,0}\oplus s_{i,1}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]\;:\;\theta[i]=1}
.

(e)

Prepare a register
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
that is initialized to

\ket{0^\secp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}}
for every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
.

(f)

For every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
, apply the map

\displaystyle\ket{u_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}}\otimes\ket{v_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}}\rightarrow\ket{u_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}}\otimes\ket{v_i\oplus s_{i,u_i}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}}

(87)

to the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
and obtain the resulting state
\rho_{i}
.

(g)

Output
\mathpzc{sk}_{y}=(\rho_{i})_{i\in{[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]}}
,

\mathsf{vk}=(x,\theta,S)
, and
\mathsf{tk}=T
.

3.

If there exists
an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
such that
F(x_{0}^{*},y)\neq F(x_{1}^{*},y)
and
V=\bot
, output
0
. Otherwise, generate

\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},x_{\mathsf{coin}}^{*})
and send
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
continues to make queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. However,
\mathpzc{A}
is not allowed to send
y
such that
F(x_{0}^{*},y)\neq F(x_{1}^{*},y)
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. Output

\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathpzc{A}
make
q={\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda)
many queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}

before the challenge phase. We will now prove the following lemma:

Lemma C.1.

\forall j\in\{0,\ldots,q-1\}
and
\mathsf{coin}\in\{0,1\}:\mathsf{Hyb}_{j}^{\mathsf{coin}}\approx_{c}\mathsf{Hyb}_{j+1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathsf{Hyb}_{j}^{\mathsf{coin}}\not\approx\mathsf{Hyb}_{j+1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
. Let
\mathpzc{D}
be
a corresponding distinguisher. We will
construct a reduction
\mathpzc{R}
that breaks the IND-CVA-CD security of
the BB84-based SKE-CD scheme
\mathsf{SKECD}
. The execution of
\mathpzc{R}{D}
in
the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKECD},\mathpzc{R}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cva}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cd}}(1^{\lambda},b)
proceeds
as follows:

Execution of
\mathpzc{R}{D}
in

\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKECD},\mathpzc{R}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cva}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cd}}(1^{\lambda},b)
:

1.

The challenger
\mathpzc{Ch}
computes
\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

\mathpzc{R}
initializes
\mathpzc{D}
with
1^{\lambda}
which outputs
(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
.

3.

\mathpzc{R}
samples
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
. It initializes a list

L_{\mathpzc{KG}}
to an empty list.

4.

\mathpzc{R}
simulates the oracle
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
for
\mathpzc{D}
as follows:

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(y):
Given
y
, it finds an entry of the form

(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
from
L_{\mathpzc{KG}}
. If there is such an entry, it returns

\bot
. Otherwise, it proceeds as follows for the
k
-th query:

(a)

If
k\leq j
and
F(x_{0}^{*},y)\neq F(x_{1}^{*},y)
, then compute
(\mathpzc{sk},\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{tk})
as in

\widetilde{\mathpzc{KG}}
except that the pair
(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{vk})
is
obtained as the output of
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Enc}}}(r)
instead. Send
\mathpzc{sk}
and

\mathsf{tk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
and add
(y,\mathsf{vk},\bot)
to
L{KG}
.

(b)

If
k=j+1
and
F(x_{0}^{*},y)\neq F(x_{1}^{*},y)
, compute

\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},y)
. Then, send
(r^{*},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y})
to
\mathpzc{Ch}
where
r^{*}
is a random value. On receiving

\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}
from
\mathpzc{Ch}
, compute
\mathpzc{sk}
and
\mathsf{tk}
as in
\mathpzc{KG}
,
except that
\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}
is used in place of
\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{ct}
.
Send
\mathpzc{sk}
and
\mathsf{tk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
and add
(y,0^{\lambda},\bot)
to

L_{\mathpzc{KG}}
.

(c)

If
k>j+1
or
F(x_{0}^{*},y)=F(x_{1}^{*},y)
, then compute

(\mathpzc{sk},\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{tk})
as in
\mathpzc{KG}
except that the pair

(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{vk})
is replaced with the output of

O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Enc}}}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y})
instead. Send
\mathpzc{sk}
and
\mathsf{tk}
to
\mathpzc{A}

and add
(y,\mathsf{vk},\bot)
to
L_{\mathpzc{KG}}
.

5.

\mathpzc{R}
simulates the oracle
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
for
\mathpzc{D}
as
follows:

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(y,\widetilde{\mathpzc{sk}}):
Given
(y,\widetilde{\mathpzc{sk}})
, it finds an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
from
L{KG}

(If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
.) It then proceeds as
follows, if
y
corresponds to the
k
-th query made to

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and
k\neq j+1
:

(a)

Parse
\mathsf{vk}=(x,\theta,S=\{s_{i,0}\oplus s_{i,1}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]\;:\;\theta[i]=1})
and
\widetilde{\mathpzc{sk}}=(\rho_{i})_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]}
.

(b)

For every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
, measure
\rho_{i}
in the Hadamard
basis to get outcomes
c_{i},d_{i}
corresponding to the registers

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
respectively.

(c)

Compute
\mathsf{cert}[i]=c_{i}\oplus d_{i}\cdot(s_{i,0}\oplus s_{i,1})
for every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
.

(d)

If
x[i]=\mathsf{cert}[i]
holds
for every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]:\theta[i]=1
, then update the entry to

(y,\mathsf{vk},\top)
and send
\top
to
\mathpzc{D}
. Else, send
\bot
.

If
k=j+1
, then it proceeds as follows:

(a)

Compute
\mathsf{cert}=\mathsf{cert}[1]\|\ldots\|\mathsf{cert}[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
where
each
\mathsf{cert}[i]
is computed as in the previous case.

(b)

Send
\mathsf{cert}
to
\mathpzc{Ch}
. If
\mathpzc{Ch}
returns
\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk}
, send

\top
to
\mathpzc{D}
and update the corresponding entry to
(y,\mathsf{vk},\top)
. Else, output
\bot
.

6.

\mathpzc{D}
requests the challenge ciphertext. If there exists
an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
such that
F(x_{0}^{*},y)\neq F(x_{1}^{*},y)
and
V=\bot
, output
0
. Otherwise, compute and send
\mathsf{ct}^{\star}=(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},x^{*}_{\mathsf{coin}}))
to
\mathpzc{D}
.

7.

\mathpzc{D}
continues to make queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. However,
\mathpzc{A}
is not allowed to send
y
such that
F(x_{0}^{*},y)\neq F(x_{1}^{*},y)
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.
Consequently,
\mathpzc{R}
simulates these queries as per Step 4. (c) above.

8.

\mathpzc{D}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
which
\mathpzc{R}

forwards to
\mathpzc{Ch}
.
\mathpzc{Ch}
outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the output of the
experiment.

We will first argue that when
b=1
, the view of
\mathpzc{D}
is exactly the
same as its view in the hybrid
\mathsf{Hyb}_{j}^{\mathsf{coin}}
. Notice that the for the
first
j
queries, if
F(x_{0}^{*},y)\neq F(x_{1}^{*},y)
for a key-query
corresponding to
y
, then the decryption key is computed by querying
the encryption oracle on a random plaintext. If this condition does
not hold, then the key is computed by querying the encryption oracle
on the corresponding SKFE key
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y}
. The hybrid
\mathsf{Hyb}_{j}
on
the other hand, directly computes these values, but there is no
difference in the distribution of the output ciphertexts. A similar
argument holds for the keys
\mathpzc{dk}_{j+2},\ldots,\mathpzc{dk}_{q}
, which
contain encryptions of the corresponding keys
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y}
. Note
that if
F(x_{0}^{*},y^{*})=F(x_{1}^{*},y^{*})
, where
y^{*}
corresponds to
the
j+1
-th query, then the hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{j}^{\mathsf{coin}}
and

\mathsf{Hyb}_{j+1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
are identical. Hence, consider the case when

F(x_{0}^{*},y^{*})\neq F(x_{1}^{*},y^{*})
. In this case, if
b=1
,
the value encrypted as part of the key
\mathpzc{sk}_{j+1}
is

\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y^{*}}
. This is the same as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{j}^{\mathsf{coin}}
. As for the
verification oracle queries, notice that they are answered similarly
by the reduction and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{j}^{\mathsf{coin}}
for all but the
j+1
-th key. For
the
j+1
-th key, the reduction works differently in that it forwards
the certificate
\mathsf{cert}
to the verification oracle. However, the
verification procedure of the BB84-based SKE-CD scheme checks the
validity of the value
\mathsf{cert}
in the same way as the reduction, so
there is no difference.
Finally, notice that when
b=0
, the encrypted value is an independent
and random value, similar to the hybrid
\mathsf{Hyb}_{j+1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
.
Consequently,
\mathpzc{R}
breaks the IND-CVA-CD security of
\mathsf{SKECD}
with
non-negligible probability, a contradiction.
∎

Notice now that the hybrid
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}
is the same as the
experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}\allowbreak(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
. From
the previous lemma, we have that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}\approx_{c}\mathsf{Hyb}_{q}^{\mathsf{coin}}
. However, we have that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{q}^{0}\approx_{c}\mathsf{Hyb}_{q}^{1}

holds from the selective single-ciphertext security of the underlying
SKFE scheme
\mathsf{SKFE}
. This is because any key-query corresponding to

y
after the
q
-th query is such that
F(x_{0}^{*},y)=F(x_{1}^{*},y)
.
For the first
q
queries, wherever this condition doesn’t hold, the
SKFE keys have been replaced with random values. Consequently, we have
that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{0}\approx_{c}\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{1}
, which completes the proof. ∎

C.3
Proof of Key-Testability

First, we will argue the correctness requirement. Recall that

\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}
applies the following map to a BB84 state

\ket{x}_{\theta}
, where
(x,\theta)\in\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}\times\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}
, for every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
:

\displaystyle\ket{u_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}}\otimes\ket{v_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}}\rightarrow\ket{u_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}}\otimes\ket{v_i\oplus s_{i,u_i}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}}

(88)

where
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}
denotes the register holding the
i
-th
qubit of
\ket{x}_{\theta}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
is a register initialized
to
\ket{0\ldots 0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}}
.

Consider applying the algorithm
\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{KeyTest}
in
superposition to the resulting state, i.e., performing the following
map, where
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{\mathsf{SKECD}.CT}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{\mathsf{SKECD}.CT_{1}}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{\mathsf{SKECD}.CT_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{1}}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}}}}
, and

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{KT}}}
is initialized to
\ket{0}
:

\displaystyle\ket{u}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT}}}}\otimes\ket{v}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S}}}}\otimes\ket{\beta}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{KT}}}}\rightarrow\ket{u}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT}}}}\otimes\ket{v}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S}}}}\otimes\ket{\beta\oplus\SKFESKL.
\KeyTest(\tk, u\|v)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{KT}}}}

(89)

where
\mathsf{tk}=T=t_{1,0}\|t_{1,1}\|\cdots\|t_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}},0}\|t_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}},1}
. Recall that
\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{KeyTest}

outputs 1 if
and only if
\mathsf{Check}[t_{i,0},t_{i,1}](u_{i},v_{i})=1
for every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
, where
u_{i},v_{i}
denote the states of the registers

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
respectively.
Recall that
\mathsf{Check}[t_{i,0},t_{i,1}](u_{i},v_{i})
computes
f(v_{i})

and checks if it equals
t_{i,u_{i}}
. Since the construction chooses

t_{i,u_{i}}
such that
f(s_{i,u_{i}})=t_{i,u_{i}}
, this check
always passes. Consequently, measuring register
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{KT}}}
always
produces outcome
1
.

We will now argue that the security requirement holds by showing the
following reduction to the security of the OWF
f
. Let
\mathpzc{A}
be an
adversary that breaks the key-testability of
\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}
. Consider
a QPT reduction
\mathpzc{R}
that works as follows in the OWF security
experiment:

Execution of
\mathpzc{R}{A}
in

\mathsf{Expt}_{f,\mathpzc{R}}^{\mathsf{owf}}(1^{\lambda})
:

1.

The challenger chooses
s\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and sends

y^{*}=f(s)
to
\mathpzc{R}
.

2.

\mathpzc{R}
runs
\mathsf{SKFE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{CR}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and initializes
\mathpzc{A}

with input
\mathsf{msk}
.

3.

\mathpzc{R}
picks a random
k^{*}\in[q]
.

4.

\mathpzc{R}
simulates the access of
\mathpzc{A}
to the oracle

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
as follows, where the list
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
is
initialized to an empty list:

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(y)
:

For the
k
-th query, do the following:

(a)

Given
y
, it finds an entry of the form

(y,\mathsf{tk})
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. If there is such an entry, it
returns
\bot
.

(b)

Otherwise, if
k\neq k^{*}
, it generates

(\mathpzc{sk}_{y},\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{tk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y)
, sends
(\mathpzc{sk}_{y},\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{tk})
to
\mathpzc{A}
,
and adds
(y,\mathsf{tk})
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

(c)

Otherwise, if
k=k^{*}
, it generates
(\mathpzc{sk}_{y},\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{tk})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathpzc{KG}}(\mathsf{msk},y)
(differences from
\mathpzc{KG}
are colored in

red
). It then sends
(\mathpzc{sk}_{y},\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{tk})
to
\mathpzc{A}
and
adds
(y,\mathsf{tk})
to
L_{\mathpzc{KG}}
.

\widetilde{\mathpzc{KG}}(\mathsf{msk},y)

(a)

Parse
\mathsf{msk}=(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk})
.

(b)

Generate
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},y)
.

(c)

Generate

(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y})
.
Here,
\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{vk}
is of the form

(x,\theta)\in\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}\times\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}
, and

\mathsf{skecd}.\mathpzc{ct}
is of the form

\ket{\psi_1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{1}}}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\ket{\psi_{\ctlen}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}}}}}
.

(d)

Choose an index
i^{\star}\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
such
that
\theta[i^{\star}]=0
. For every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
such
that
i\neq i^{\star}
, generate
s_{i,b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and compute

t_{i,b}\leftarrow f(s_{i,b})
for every
b\in\{0,1\}
. For
i=i^{\star}
,
set
t_{i^{\star},1-x[i^{\star}]}=y^{*}
. Then, generate
s_{i^{\star},x[i^{\star}]}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and compute
t_{i^{\star},x[i^{\star}]}=f(s_{i^{\star},x[i^{\star}]})
.

Set
T\coloneqq t_{1,0}\|t_{1,1}\|\cdots\|t_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}},0}\|t_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}},1}
and
S=\{s_{i,0}\oplus s_{i,1}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]\;:\;\theta[i]=1}
.

(e)

Prepare a register
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
that is initialized to

\ket{0^\secp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}}
for every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
.

(f)

For every
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]
, apply the map

\displaystyle\ket{u_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}}\otimes\ket{v_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}}\rightarrow\ket{u_i}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}}\otimes\ket{v_i\oplus s_{i,u_i}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}}

(90)

to the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{i}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
and obtain the resulting state
\rho_{i}
.

(g)

Output
\mathpzc{sk}_{y}=(\rho_{i})_{i\in{[\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}]}}
,

\mathsf{vk}=(x,\theta,S)
, and
\mathsf{tk}=T
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}
sends a tuple of classical strings
(y,\mathsf{sk},x^{*})
to
\mathpzc{R}
.

\mathpzc{R}
outputs
\bot
if there is no entry of the form
(y,\mathsf{tk})
in

L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
for some
\mathsf{tk}
. Also, if
k\neq k^{*}
,
\mathpzc{R}
outputs
\bot
.
Otherwise,
\mathpzc{R}
parses
\mathsf{sk}

as a string over the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{1}}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{1}}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}}}}
and measures the register

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i^{\star}}}}}
to obtain an outcome
s_{i^{\star}}
.
\mathpzc{R}

then sends
s_{i^{\star}}
to the challenger.

Notice that the view of
\mathpzc{A}
is the same as its view in the
key-testability experiment, as only the value
t_{i^{\star},1-x[i^{\star}]}
is generated differently by forwarding the value
y
,
but this value is distributed identically to the original value.
Note that in both cases,
\mathpzc{A}
receives no information about a
pre-image of
t_{i^{\star},1-x[i^{\star}]}
.
Now,
\mathpzc{R}
guesses the index
k
that
\mathpzc{A}
targets with probability

\frac{1}{q}
. By assumption, we have that
\mathsf{CDec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct})\neq F(x^{*},y)
where
\mathsf{ct}=\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},x^{*})
. The value
\mathsf{sk}
can be
parsed as a string over the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S}}}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathsf{sk}}
be the sub-string of
\mathsf{sk}
on the
register
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{SKECD.CT}}}
. Recall that
\mathsf{CDec}
invokes the
algorithm
\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{CDec}
on input
\widetilde{\mathsf{sk}}
. We will
now recall a property of
\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{CDec}
that was specified in
Definition
3.12
:

Let
(\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{vk}=(x,\theta))\leftarrow\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y})

where
\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
. Now, let
u
be any
arbitrary value such that
u[i]=x[i]
for all
i:\theta[i]=0
.
Then, the following holds:

\Pr[\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{CDec}(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},u)=\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y}\Big]\geq 1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

Consequently, if
\widetilde{\mathsf{sk}}
is such that
\widetilde{\mathsf{sk}}[i]=x[i]
for all
i:\theta[i]=0
, where
(x,\theta)
are
specified by
\mathsf{vk}_{k^{\star}}
, then
\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{CDec}(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\widetilde{\mathsf{sk}})
outputs the value
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{y}
with high
probability. Since
\mathsf{CDec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}=\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}\allowbreak(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},x^{*})))
outputs

\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{SKECD}.\mathsf{CDec}(\mathsf{skecd}.\mathsf{sk},\widetilde{\mathsf{sk}}),\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct})
, we
have that it outputs
x^{*}
with high probability from the decryption
correctness of
\mathsf{SKFE}
. Therefore, it must be the case that there
exists some index
i
for which
\widetilde{\mathsf{sk}}\neq x[i]
. With
probability
\frac{1}{\ell_{\mathsf{ct}}}
, this happens to be the guessed value

i^{\star}
. In this case,
\mathpzc{A}
must output
s_{i^{\star}}
on register

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{S_{i}}}}
such that
f(s_{i^{\star}})=t_{i^{\star},1-x[i^{\star}]}=y^{*}
. This concludes the proof. ∎

Since we have proved selective single-ciphertext security and
key-testability, we can now state the following theorem:

Theorem C.2.

Assuming the existence of a BB84-based SKE-CD scheme and the existence
of OWFs, there exists a selective single-ciphertext KLA secure
SKFE-CR-SKL scheme satisfying the key-testability property.
